Deformations and Simulatenous Resolutions of Determinantal Surfaces
Brian Makonzi

Abstract
. This paper uses reconstruction algebras to construct simultaneous resolution of determinantal surfaces. The main new difference to the classical case is that, in addition to the quiver of the reconstruction algebra, certain noncommutative relations, namely those of the canonical algebra of Ringel, are required. All the relations of the reconstruction algebra except the canonical relation are then deformed, and these deformed relations together with variation of the GIT quotient achieve the simultaneous resolution.



1.
introduction

Rational surface singularities play a fundamental role in various areas of mathematics, such as algebraic geometry and singularity theory. In this paper, we specifically focus on constructing simultaneous resolution using noncommutative techniques, in the case of determinantal singularities. These are both non-Gorenstein and non-toric. The noncommutative framework we employ enables us to explore a wider spectrum of rational surface singularities, facilitating a deeper understanding of their properties and paving the way for future investigations.

1.1.
Motivation and Background

Grothendieck and Brieskorn
[
B1
]

[
B2
]
made significant contributions in constructing the deformation space for Kleinian singularities
\mathbb{C}^{2}/H
with
H\leq\mathrm{SL}(2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\mathbb{C}).
They established a connection between these singularities and the Weyl group
W
of the corresponding simple simply–connected complex Lie group. Brieskorn
[
B2
]
successfully constructed the versal deformation
D\to\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbb{C}}/W
for a rational double point, and after base change via the action of the Weyl group as in the diagram below, the resulting space
Art
resolves simultaneously
[
B2
]
.

Art
D
X
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbb{C}}
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbb{C}}/W

Kronheimer
[
K
]
and Cassens-Slodowy
[
CS
, §3]
introduced the utilization of the McKay quiver as a means to construct the semiuniversal deformation of Kleinian singularities, along with their simultaneous resolutions, belonging to types
A_{n}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}~D_{n}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}~E_{6}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}~E_{7}
and
E_{8}
. Building upon this, Crawley-Boevey and Holland
[
CH
]
later provided a reinterpretation of this approach in the context of the deformed preprojective algebra.

However, for non-Gorenstein surface quotient singularities, namely
those
\mathbb{C}^{2}/H
for small finite groups
H\leq\mathrm{GL}(2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\mathbb{C})
that are not inside
\mathrm{SL}(2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\mathbb{C})
, the situation poses a more formidable challenge. Artin
[
A
]
revealed that within the deformation space of non-Gorenstein singularities, there exists a distinct component(the Artin component) which is irreducible and exhibits the
intriguing property of admitting simultaneous resolution, again after a finite base change by some appropriate Weyl group
W
.

Art
D
X
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathbb{C}}
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathbb{C}}/W

Riemenschneider
[
R1
]
computed the Artin component
Art
for cyclic quotient singularities, then later in
[
R2
, §5]
he used the McKay quiver and special representations as described by Wunram
[
W2
]
to give an alternative description. But simultaneous resolution is also not obtained using the McKay quiver perspective. Our previous work
[
M1
]
solves this by using reconstruction algebras to construct simultaneous resolution for cyclic groups.

Building upon this success, this paper extends these techniques to determinantal surface singularities, thereby broadening the scope of known examples with simultaneous resolutions.

1.2.
Main Result

This paper considers a class of rational surface singularities called determinantal surface singularities. Consider
R\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
the determinantal singularity given as the quotient of
\mathbb{C}[v\mathchar 59\relax w_{1}\mathchar 59\relax w_{2}\mathchar 59\relax w_{3}]
by the
2\times 2
minors of the matrix

\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}{w}_{2}&{w}_{3}&{v^{p_{2}}}\\
{v^{p_{1}}}&{{w}_{3}+{v^{p_{3}}}}&{w}_{1}\end{array}\right).

The minimal resolution
X\rightarrow\text{Spec}~R
is the star-shaped graph in (
2.A
).

The quiver of the reconstruction algebra corresponding to Spec
R
is recalled in §
2
, and will be written
Q.
For dimension vector
\updelta=(1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\ldots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1)\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
consider
\mathcal{R}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathbb{C}[\Rep(\mathbb{C}Q\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\updelta)]/I
and crucially
I
is the ideal generated by Ringel’s
[
R3
]
canonical relation (see (
2.B
)). This carries a natural action of
G\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\textstyle\prod_{q\in Q_{0}}\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
where
Q_{0}
denotes the set of vertices of
Q.
As shown in §
4.2
,
\mathcal{R}^{G}
is generated by cycles. These generate a
\mathbb{C}
–algebra
\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{w}\mathchar 59\relax\mathsf{v}]\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
and they further satisfy determinantal relations (recalled in §
5.1
).

Simultaneous resolution is then achieved by introducing the deformed reconstruction algebra (see
3.3
), which generalises the work of
Crawley-Boevey–Holland
[
CH
]
on deformed preprojective algebras. In §
4.3
, we construct a map
\uppi\colon\mathcal{R}^{G}\rightarrow\Delta\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
where
\Delta
is an affine space defined in Notation
3.5
. The fibre above the origin is the determinantal singularity Spec
R
above (see Remark
4.4
later). The following is our main result, where
\upvartheta_{0}
is a
particular
choice of stability condition explained in §
3.2
.

Theorem 1.1

(
4.5
)
.

The diagram

\Rep(\mathbb{C}Q/I\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}~\updelta)~/\!\!\!\!/_{\upvartheta_{0}}\mathrm{GL}
\mathcal{\mathcal{R}}^{G}
\Delta
\upphi
\uppi

is a simultaneous resolution of singularities in the sense that the morphism
\upphi
is smooth, and
\uppi
is flat.

The smoothness of the fibres is achieved using moduli spaces of the deformed reconstruction algebra
\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{\upgamma}}.
These are introduced in §
3.3
, and may be of independent interest. Note that this is significantly more difficult than for cyclic quotients, with the main difficulty being showing that
\mathcal{M}_{\upvartheta_{0}}\neq\emptyset
(see Remark
3.7
).

This paper is organised as follows. Section
2
recalls the reconstruction algebra of star-shaped graphs. Section
3
introduces the deformed reconstruction algebra, and uses this in section
4
to achieve simultaneous resolution. Section
5

gives a conjectural presentation of
\mathcal{\mathcal{R}}^{G}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
although this is not required for simultaneous resolution.

Conventions

Throughout we work over the complex numbers
\mathbb{C}.
If
a
and
b
are arrows in a quiver,
ab
denotes
a
followed by
b\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

t(a)
is the tail of
a
and
h(a)
is the head of
a.

preliminaries

(2.A)

where there are
3
arms, and the number of vertices on arm
i
is
p_{i}-1
. Contracting these curves gives the singularity Spec
R
in §
1.2
. To (
2.A
) we associate the extended quiver, and the extended vertex is called the
0^{th}
vertex. The resulting quiver is as shown below

The notation
d_{12}
is read ‘the second downward arrow along arm 1’.

From this, Ringel
[
R3
]
introduces the canonical algebra, as the path algebra of the quiver
Q^{\prime}
subject to the relation

I=\left<d_{11}\ldots d_{1p_{1}}-d_{21}\ldots d_{2p_{2}}+d_{31}\ldots d_{3p_{3}}\right>.

The double quiver of the above is written as follows

(2.B)

Set
D_{i}=d_{i1}\ldots d_{ip_{i}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

U_{i}=u_{ip_{i}}\ldots u_{i1}
, which are the paths down, respectively up, arm
i
.

Definition 2.1
.

The reconstruction algebra is defined to be the path algebra of
Q\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
subject to the relations induced by the canonical relations, and at every vertex, all 2-cycles that exist at that vertex are equal. Thus, the recontruction algebra can be written explicitly as the following relations.

\displaystyle u_{1i}d_{1i}

\displaystyle=d_{1i+1}u_{1i+1}~\text{for all}~1\leq i\leq p_{1}-1

\displaystyle u_{2i}d_{2i}

\displaystyle=d_{2i+1}u_{2i+1}~\text{for all}~1\leq i\leq p_{2}-1

\displaystyle u_{3i}d_{3i}

\displaystyle=d_{3i+1}u_{3i+1}~\text{for all}~1\leq i\leq p_{3}-1

\displaystyle d_{21}u_{21}

\displaystyle=d_{11}u_{11}

\displaystyle d_{21}u_{21}

\displaystyle=d_{31}u_{31}

\displaystyle u_{1p_{1}}d_{1p_{1}}

\displaystyle=u_{2p_{2}}d_{2p_{2}}

\displaystyle u_{3p_{3}}d_{3p_{3}}

\displaystyle=u_{2p_{2}}d_{2p_{2}}

\displaystyle D_{1}-D_{2}

\displaystyle+D_{3}=0.

3.
The Moduli Space

In this section, the moduli space of both the reconstruction algebra and the deformed reconstruction algebra is computed. This will be used to achieve simultaneous resolution in §
4
.

3.1.
Generalities

Consider the dimension vector
\updelta=(1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\ldots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1)
and the representation variety
\Rep(\mathbb{C}Q\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\updelta)
, where
Q
is the quiver in (
2.B
). We consider
\Rep(\mathbb{C}Q\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\updelta)
as an affine space, and write

\mathcal{R}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\frac{\mathbb{C}[\Rep(\mathbb{C}Q\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\updelta)]}{\left<D_{1}-D_{2}+D_{3}\right>\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}}

(3.A)

which we identify with the polynomial ring in the number of arrow variables subject to the relation

I=\left<D_{1}-D_{2}+D_{3}\right>.

The co-ordinate ring carries a natural action of
G\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\textstyle\prod_{q\in Q_{0}}\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
where
Q_{0}
denotes the set of vertices of
Q
. The action is via conjugation, namely
\upmu\in G=\mathbb{C}^{\ast}\times\ldots\times\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
acts on
p+I\in\mathcal{R}
as
\upmu\cdot(p+I)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\upmu\cdot p+I=\upmu^{-1}_{t(p)}p\upmu_{h(p)}+I
.

3.2.
Moduli of Quiver with one relation

With respect to the ordering of the vertices as in Section
2
, this subsection computes the moduli space of the quiver
Q
in §
2
subject to the single relation
I=\left<D_{1}-D_{2}+D_{3}\right>\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
under the dimension vector
\updelta=(1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax 1)\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
and generic King stability condition
\upvartheta_{0}=(-(m_{1}+m_{2}+m_{3}+1)\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax 1).

Set
\mathcal{M}_{\upvartheta_{0}}:=\mathcal{R}~/\!\!\!\!/_{\upvartheta_{0}}\mathrm{GL}
where
\mathcal{R}
is defined in (
3.A
), and consider the following open subsets of
\mathcal{M}_{\upvartheta_{0}}
with either
D_{1}\mathchar 59\relax~D_{2}~\text{or}~D_{3}\neq 0
where
D_{i}\coloneqq d_{i1}\ldots d_{ip_{i}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

V^{1}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cccc}D_{1}\neq 0\mathchar 59\relax&d_{21}\ldots d_{2i}\neq 0\mathchar 59\relax&u_{2p_{2}}\ldots u_{2{i+2}}\neq 0\\
&d_{31}\ldots d_{3j}\neq 0\mathchar 59\relax&u_{3p_{3}}\ldots u_{3{j+2}}\neq 0\end{array}\right\}0\leq i\leq p_{2}-1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}~0\leq j\leq p_{3}-1\mathchar 59\relax

V^{2}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cccc}D_{2}\neq 0\mathchar 59\relax&d_{11}\ldots d_{1i}\neq 0\mathchar 59\relax&u_{1p_{1}}\ldots u_{1{i+2}}\neq 0\\
&d_{31}\ldots d_{3j}\neq 0\mathchar 59\relax&u_{3p_{3}}\ldots u_{3{j+2}}\neq 0\\
\end{array}\right\}0\leq i\leq p_{1}-1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}~0\leq j\leq p_{3}-1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

V^{3}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cccc}D_{3}\neq 0\mathchar 59\relax&d_{11}\ldots d_{1i}\neq 0\mathchar 59\relax&u_{1p_{1}}\ldots u_{1{i+2}}\neq 0\\
&d_{21}\ldots d_{2j}\neq 0\mathchar 59\relax&u_{2p_{2}}\ldots u_{2{j+2}}\neq 0\\
\end{array}\right\}0\leq i\leq p_{1}-1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}~0\leq j\leq p_{2}-1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

where by convention, if a product does not exist (e.g
d_{21}\ldots d_{2i}
with
i=0\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
or
u_{3p_{3}}\ldots u_{3j+2}
with
j=p_{3}-1
), then that condition is empty.

Lemma 3.1
.

With notation as above, the open sets
V^{1}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}\mathchar 59\relax V^{2}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}\mathchar 59\relax V^{3}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}
completely cover the moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{\upvartheta_{0}}
.

Proof.

By
[
W1
, Remark 3.10]
, a
\mathbb{C}Q/I
module
M
of dimension vector
(1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax 1)
is
\upvartheta_{0}
-stable if and only if
for every vertex in the quiver representation of
M
, there exists a non-zero path from the zero vertex to that vertex. So, if we consider the bottom vertex of
M\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
we must have either
D_{1}\neq 0\mathchar 59\relax~D_{2}\neq 0
or
D_{3}\neq 0.
By symmetry, suppose that
D_{1}\neq 0.
For convenience in the argument below, label the arrows and vertices of
Q
as follows

If
d_{21}=0
and
d_{31}=0
then the only way a non-zero path can reach vertex
(2m_{2})
and vertex
(3m_{3})
is if
D_{1}u_{2p_{2}}\ldots u_{22}\neq 0
and
D_{1}u_{3p_{3}}\ldots u_{32}\neq 0
respectively. In this case
M
belongs to
V^{1}_{0\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}0}.
If
d_{21}\ldots d_{2p_{2}-1}\neq 0
and
d_{31}\ldots d_{3p_{3}-1}\neq 0
then by a similar argument
M
is in
V^{1}_{p_{2}-1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}p_{3}-1}.
Hence we can assume that some
d
on arm 2, and some
d
on arm 3 is equal to zero. In each arm, consider the
d
closest to the top vertex which is zero: thus

\displaystyle d_{21}\ldots d_{2i}\neq 0

\displaystyle~\text{and}~d_{2i+1}=0

\displaystyle d_{31}\ldots d_{3j}\neq 0

\displaystyle~\text{and}~d_{3j+1}=0\mathchar 59\relax

and therefore
M
is in
V^{1}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}.

∎

From now on, to ease notation set
\mathbb{C}[U^{k}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}]=\mathbb{C}[V^{k}_{i-1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j-1}]
for
k=1\mathchar 59\relax 2\mathchar 59\relax 3.

Proposition 3.2
.

With notation as above, the following statements hold.

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}[U^{1}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}]\cong\frac{\mathbb{C}[u_{11}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax u_{1p_{1}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}u_{21}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax u_{2i-1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}u_{2i}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}d_{2i}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax d_{2p_{2}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}u_{31}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax u_{3j-1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}u_{3j}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}d_{3j}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax d_{3p_{3}}]}{\left(1-d_{2i}\ldots d_{2p_{2}}+d_{3j}\ldots d_{3p_{3}}\right)}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}[U^{2}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}]\cong\frac{\mathbb{C}[u_{21}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax u_{2p_{2}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}u_{11}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax u_{1i-1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}u_{1i}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}d_{1i}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax d_{1p_{1}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}u_{31}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax u_{3j-1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}u_{3j}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}d_{3j}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax d_{3p_{3}}]}{\left(d_{1i}\ldots d_{1p_{1}}-1+d_{3j}\ldots d_{3p_{3}}\right)}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}[U^{3}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}]\cong\frac{\mathbb{C}[u_{31}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax u_{3p_{3}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}u_{11}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax u_{1i-1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}u_{1i}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}d_{1i}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax d_{1p_{1}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}u_{21}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax u_{2j-1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}u_{2j}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}d_{2j}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax d_{2p_{2}}]}{\left(d_{1i}\ldots d_{1p_{1}}-d_{2j}\ldots d_{2p_{2}}+1\right)}.

Proof.

Case 1.
For
U^{1}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
after changing basis we set the specified non-zero arrows to be 1. Therefore,
the only possible non-identity arrows on arm 1 are

u_{11}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax u_{1p_{1}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

the only possible non-identity arrows on arm 2 are

u_{21}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax u_{2i-1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}u_{2i}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}d_{2i}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}d_{2i+1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax d_{2p_{2}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

and the only possible non-identity arrows on arm 3 are

u_{31}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax u_{3j-1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}u_{3j}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}d_{3j}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}d_{3j+1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax d_{3p_{3}}.

Since

D_{1}=1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}~D_{2}=d_{2i}d_{2i+1}\ldots d_{2p_{2}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}~D_{3}=d_{3j}d_{3j+1}\ldots d_{3p_{3}}
the only relation is
1-d_{2i}\ldots d_{2p_{2}}+d_{3j}\ldots d_{3p_{3}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
as claimed.

Case 2.
The proof of
\mathbb{C}[U^{2}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}]
and
\mathbb{C}[U^{3}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}]
are similar to that of
\mathbb{C}[U^{1}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}]
above.
∎

Corollary 3.3
.

\mathcal{M}_{\upvartheta_{0}}
is smooth.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.1
,
\mathcal{M}_{\upvartheta_{0}}
is covered by the open sets
U^{1}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}\mathchar 59\relax U^{2}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}\mathchar 59\relax U^{3}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
so it suffices to prove that each of these is smooth. By Lemma
3.2
, set
f=1-d_{2i}\ldots d_{2p_{2}}+d_{3j}\ldots d_{3p_{3}}
and consider the ideal

K=\left(f\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}~\frac{\partial f}{\partial v}\mid v~\text{is variable in}~\mathbb{C}[U^{1}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}]\right).

Since clearly
1\in K\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
as is standard
[
GP
, Algorithm 5.7.6]
, this implies that
\mathbb{C}[U^{1}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}]
is smooth. The proof for
\mathbb{C}[U^{2}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}]
and
\mathbb{C}[U^{3}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}]
is identical.
Thus,
\mathcal{M}_{\upvartheta_{0}}
is smooth.
∎

3.3.
The Deformed Reconstruction Algebras

In this subsection we introduce a deformed version of the reconstruction algebra, and compute its moduli space.

Definition 3.4
.

Given scalars
\boldsymbol{\upgamma}\in\mathbb{C}^{\oplus p_{1}-1}\oplus\mathbb{C}^{\oplus p_{2}-1}\oplus\mathbb{C}^{\oplus p_{3}-1}\oplus\mathbb{C}^{\oplus 4}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
write
\boldsymbol{\upgamma}=(\boldsymbol{\upgamma}_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\boldsymbol{\upgamma}_{2}\mathchar 59\relax\boldsymbol{\upgamma}_{3}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

A\mathchar 59\relax B\mathchar 59\relax a\mathchar 59\relax b)~\text{where}~\boldsymbol{\upgamma}_{1}=(\upgamma_{11}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\upgamma_{1p_{1}-1})\mathchar 59\relax\boldsymbol{\upgamma}_{2}=(\upgamma_{21}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\upgamma_{2p_{2}-1})\mathchar 59\relax\boldsymbol{\upgamma}_{3}=(\upgamma_{31}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\upgamma_{3p_{3}-1}).
Then the deformed reconstruction algebra
\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{\upgamma}}
is defined to be the path algebra of the quiver
Q\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
subject to the following relations.

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}(1)&~u_{1i}d_{1i}-d_{1i+1}u_{1i+1}&=&\upgamma_{1i}&~\text{for all}~1\leq i\leq p_{1}-1\\
(2)&~u_{2i}d_{2i}-d_{2i+1}u_{2i+1}&=&\upgamma_{2i}&~\text{for all}~1\leq i\leq p_{2}-1\\
(3)&~u_{3i}d_{3i}-d_{3i+1}u_{3i+1}&=&\upgamma_{3i}&~\text{for all}~1\leq i\leq p_{3}-1\\
(a)&~d_{21}u_{21}-d_{11}u_{11}&=&a&\\
(b)&~d_{21}u_{21}-d_{31}u_{31}&=&b&\\
(c)&~u_{1p_{1}}d_{1p_{1}}-u_{2p_{2}}d_{2p_{2}}&=&A&\\
(d)&~u_{3p_{3}}d_{3p_{3}}-u_{2p_{2}}d_{2p_{2}}&=&B&\\
(x)&~d_{11}\ldots d_{1p_{1}}-d_{21}\ldots d_{2p_{2}}+d_{31}\ldots d_{3p_{3}}&=&0.&\end{array}

Note that
\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{0}}
is the reconstruction algebra defined in §
2
earlier.

Notation 3.5
.

Set

\Delta:=\{\boldsymbol{\upgamma}\in\mathbb{C}^{\oplus p_{1}+p_{2}+p_{3}+1}\mid\sum_{i=1}^{p_{1}-1}\upgamma_{1i}-\sum_{i=1}^{p_{2}-1}\upgamma_{2i}+A+a=0~\text{and}~\sum_{i=1}^{p_{3}-1}\upgamma_{3i}-\sum_{i=1}^{p_{2}-1}\upgamma_{2i}+B+b=0\}.

Lemma 3.6
.

If
\boldsymbol{\upgamma}\in\Delta\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
then
\sum_{i=1}^{p_{1}-1}\upgamma_{1i}-\sum_{i=1}^{p_{3}-1}\upgamma_{3i}+(A-B)+(a-b)=0.

Proof.

For
\boldsymbol{\upgamma}\in\Delta\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

\sum_{i=1}^{p_{1}-1}\upgamma_{1i}-\sum_{i=1}^{p_{2}-1}\upgamma_{2i}+A+a=\sum_{i=1}^{p_{3}-1}\upgamma_{3i}-\sum_{i=1}^{p_{2}-1}\upgamma_{2i}+B+b=0

and hence the result follows.
∎

Remark 3.7
.

If
\boldsymbol{\upgamma}\notin\Delta\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
then
\Rep(\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{\upgamma}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}~\updelta)=\emptyset.
Certainly, given
\boldsymbol{\upgamma}\notin\Delta\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
then either
\sum_{i=1}^{p_{1}-1}\upgamma_{1i}-\sum_{i=1}^{p_{2}-1}\upgamma_{2i}+A+a\neq 0~\text{or}~\sum_{i=1}^{p_{3}-1}\upgamma_{3i}-\sum_{i=1}^{p_{2}-1}\upgamma_{2i}+B+b\neq 0.
If
M\in\Rep(\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{\upgamma}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}~\updelta)\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
then its linear maps between vertices are scalars, which satisfy the relations of
\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{\upgamma}}.
Now these scalars commute, and as a result of summing up the relations
(1)-(2)+(a)+(c)
gives
\sum_{i=1}^{p_{1}-1}\upgamma_{1i}-\sum_{i=1}^{p_{2}-1}\upgamma_{2i}+A+a=0
and summing the relations
(3)-(2)+(b)+(d)
gives
\sum_{i=1}^{p_{3}-1}\upgamma_{3i}-\sum_{i=1}^{p_{2}-1}\upgamma_{2i}+B+b=0\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
which is a contradiction. This is why below we always assume that
\boldsymbol{\upgamma}\in\Delta.

Now consider
\mathcal{M}_{\upvartheta_{0}}(\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{\upgamma}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}~\updelta)\coloneqq\Rep(\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{\upgamma}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}~\updelta)~/\!\!\!\!/_{\upvartheta_{0}}~\mathrm{GL}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
and define open subsets
V^{1}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}\mathchar 59\relax V^{2}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}\mathchar 59\relax V^{3}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}
in an identical manner to §
3.2
.

Proposition 3.8
.

If
\boldsymbol{\upgamma}\in\Delta\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
then the following open sets
U^{1}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}\mathchar 59\relax U^{2}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}\mathchar 59\relax U^{3}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
cover the moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{\upvartheta_{0}}(\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{\upgamma}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}~\updelta)

\mathbb{C}[U^{1}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}]\cong\frac{\mathbb{C}[d_{2i}\mathchar 59\relax u_{2i}\mathchar 59\relax d_{3j}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}u_{3j}]}{\left(\begin{array}[]{c}d_{2i}u_{2i}+\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{i-1}\upgamma_{2l}-d_{3j}u_{3j}-\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{j-1}\upgamma_{3l}-b\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\\
1-d_{2i}(d_{2i}u_{2i}-\upgamma_{2i})\ldots(d_{2i}u_{2i}-\displaystyle\sum_{l=i}^{p_{2}-1}\upgamma_{2l})+d_{3j}(d_{3j}u_{3j}-\upgamma_{3j})\ldots(d_{3j}u_{3j}-\displaystyle\sum_{l=j}^{p_{3}-1}\upgamma_{3l})\end{array}\right)}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

\mathbb{C}[U^{2}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}]\cong\frac{\mathbb{C}[d_{1i}\mathchar 59\relax u_{1i}\mathchar 59\relax d_{3j}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}u_{3j}]}{\left(\begin{array}[]{c}d_{1i}u_{1i}+\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{i-1}\upgamma_{1l}-d_{3j}u_{3j}-\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{j-1}\upgamma_{3l}-(b-a)\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\\
d_{1i}(d_{1i}u_{1i}-\upgamma_{1i})\ldots(d_{1i}u_{1i}-\displaystyle\sum_{l=i}^{p_{1}-1}\upgamma_{1l})-1+d_{3j}(d_{3j}u_{3j}-\upgamma_{3j})\ldots(d_{3j}u_{3j}-\displaystyle\sum_{l=j}^{p_{3}-1}\upgamma_{3l})\end{array}\right)}\mathchar 59\relax

\mathbb{C}[U^{3}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}]\cong\frac{\mathbb{C}[d_{1i}\mathchar 59\relax u_{1i}\mathchar 59\relax d_{2j}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}u_{2j}]}{\left(\begin{array}[]{c}d_{2j}u_{2j}+\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{j-1}\upgamma_{2l}-d_{1i}u_{1i}-\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{i-1}\upgamma_{1l}-a\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\\
d_{1i}(d_{1i}u_{1i}-\upgamma_{1i})\ldots(d_{1i}u_{1i}-\displaystyle\sum_{l=i}^{p_{1}-1}\upgamma_{1l})-d_{2j}(d_{2j}u_{2j}-\upgamma_{2j})\ldots(d_{2j}u_{2j}-\displaystyle\sum_{l=j}^{p_{2}-1}\upgamma_{2l})+1\end{array}\right)}.

Proof.

The fact these cover the moduli space is identical to Lemma
3.1
. To compute these opens for
\mathbb{C}[U^{1}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}]\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
consider the relations in Definition
3.4
. Then the relations
(1)
become

u_{1i}-u_{1i+1}=\upgamma_{1i}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\\

which implies that

\displaystyle u_{12}

\displaystyle=u_{11}-\upgamma_{11}

\displaystyle u_{13}

\displaystyle=u_{11}-\upgamma_{11}-\upgamma_{12}

\displaystyle u_{1p_{1}}

\displaystyle=u_{11}-\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{p_{1}-1}\upgamma_{1l}.

Therefore, the arrows
u_{1i}
can be expressed in terms of
u_{11}.

The relations
(2)
become

\displaystyle u_{21}-u_{22}

\displaystyle=\upgamma_{21}

\displaystyle u_{22}-u_{23}

\displaystyle=\upgamma_{22}

\displaystyle u_{2i}d_{2i}-d_{2i+1}

\displaystyle=\upgamma_{2i}

\displaystyle d_{2p_{2}-2}-d_{2p_{2}-1}

\displaystyle=\upgamma_{2p_{2}-2}

\displaystyle d_{2p_{2}-1}-d_{2p_{2}}

\displaystyle=\upgamma_{2p_{2}-1}.

Re-writing the middle as
d_{2i+1}=u_{2i}d_{2i}-\upgamma_{2i}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
working up and down gives

\displaystyle u_{21}

\displaystyle=u_{2i}d_{2i}+\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{i-1}\upgamma_{2l}

\displaystyle u_{2i-1}

\displaystyle=u_{2i}d_{2i}+\upgamma_{2i-1}

\displaystyle d_{2i+1}

\displaystyle=u_{2i}d_{2i}-\upgamma_{2i}

\displaystyle d_{2i+2}

\displaystyle=u_{2i}d_{2i}-\upgamma_{2i}-\upgamma_{2i+1}

\displaystyle d_{2p_{2}}

\displaystyle=u_{2i}d_{2i}-\displaystyle\sum_{l=i}^{p_{2}-1}\upgamma_{2l}\mathchar 59\relax

and so all the arrows in arm 2 are determined by
(u_{2i}\mathchar 59\relax d_{2i}).
In a similar way, the relations
(3)
become

\displaystyle u_{31}

\displaystyle=u_{3j}d_{3j}+\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{j-1}\upgamma_{3l}

\displaystyle u_{3j-1}

\displaystyle=u_{3j}d_{3j}+\upgamma_{3j-1}

\displaystyle d_{3j+1}

\displaystyle=u_{3j}d_{3j}-\upgamma_{3j}

\displaystyle d_{3j+2}

\displaystyle=u_{3j}d_{3j}-\upgamma_{3j}-\upgamma_{3j+1}

\displaystyle d_{3p_{3}}

\displaystyle=u_{3j}d_{3j}-\displaystyle\sum_{l=j}^{p_{3}-1}\upgamma_{3l}\mathchar 59\relax

and all the arrows in arm 3 are determined by
(u_{3j}\mathchar 59\relax d_{3j}).

The remaining relations

\displaystyle d_{21}u_{21}-d_{11}u_{11}

\displaystyle=a

\displaystyle d_{21}u_{21}-d_{31}u_{31}

\displaystyle=b

\displaystyle u_{1p_{1}}d_{1p_{1}}-u_{2p_{2}}d_{2p_{2}}

\displaystyle=A

\displaystyle u_{3p_{3}}d_{3p_{3}}-u_{2p_{2}}d_{2p_{2}}

\displaystyle=B

\displaystyle d_{11}\ldots d_{1p_{1}}-d_{21}\ldots d_{2p_{2}}

\displaystyle+d_{31}\ldots d_{3p_{3}}=0\mathchar 59\relax

then become

\displaystyle u_{2i}d_{2i}+\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{i-1}\upgamma_{2l}-u_{11}

\displaystyle=a\hskip 85.35826pt\hfill{(a)}

\displaystyle(u_{2i}d_{2i}+\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{i-1}\upgamma_{2l})-(u_{3j}d_{3j}+\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{j-1}\upgamma_{3l})

\displaystyle=b\hskip 86.78099pt\hfill{(b)}

\displaystyle(u_{11}-\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{p_{1}-1}\upgamma_{1l})-(u_{2i}d_{2i}-\displaystyle\sum_{l=i}^{p_{2}-1}\upgamma_{2l})

\displaystyle=A\hskip 85.35826pt\hfill{(c)}

\displaystyle(u_{3j}d_{3j}-\displaystyle\sum_{l=j}^{p_{3}-1}\upgamma_{3l})-(u_{2i}d_{2i}-\displaystyle\sum_{l=i}^{p_{2}-1}\upgamma_{2l})

\displaystyle=B\hskip 85.35826pt\hfill{(d)}

1-d_{2i}(d_{2i}u_{2i}-\upgamma_{2i})\ldots(d_{2i}u_{2i}-\displaystyle\sum_{l=i}^{p_{2}-1}\upgamma_{2l})+d_{3j}(d_{3j}u_{3j}-\upgamma_{3j})\ldots(d_{3j}u_{3j}-\displaystyle\sum_{l=j}^{p_{3}-1}\upgamma_{3l})=0.\hfill{(x)}

Since
\boldsymbol{\upgamma}\in\Delta\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

(a)+(c)=0
and
(b)+(d)=0.
Thus
(a)
and
(b)
are satisfied automatically from
(c)
and
(d).
By Lemma
3.5

(c)-(d)=0\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
so from
(b)
and
(x)
all relations are satisfied.

The proof of
\mathbb{C}[U^{2}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}]
and
\mathbb{C}[U^{3}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}]
are similar to that of
\mathbb{C}[U^{1}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}]
above.
∎

The following two results will be used in showing that
\mathcal{M}_{\upvartheta_{0}}(\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{\upgamma}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}~\updelta)
is smooth.

Lemma 3.9
.

In
\mathbb{C}[x\mathchar 59\relax y]\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
for any
f=x(xy-\upalpha_{1})\ldots(xy-\upalpha_{n})
with
\upalpha_{i}\in\mathbb{C}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

x\frac{\partial f}{\partial x}-y\frac{\partial f}{\partial y}=f(x\mathchar 59\relax y).

Proof.

Set
h_{i}=(xy-\upalpha_{i})
for
i=1\mathchar 59\relax 2\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax n\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
and

g\coloneqq(xy-\upalpha_{1})\ldots(xy-\upalpha_{n})=h_{1}\ldots h_{n}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

so that
f=xg.
Using the product rule,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial f}{\partial x}=g+x\cdot\frac{\partial g}{\partial x}

\displaystyle\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{\partial f}{\partial y}=x\cdot\frac{\partial g}{\partial y}.

Thus the statement follows if
x^{2}\cdot\frac{\partial g}{\partial x}-xy\cdot\frac{\partial g}{\partial y}=0\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
which holds provided that

x\cdot\frac{\partial g}{\partial x}-y\cdot\frac{\partial g}{\partial y}=0.
Now, again by the product rule

\displaystyle x\cdot\frac{\partial g}{\partial x}

\displaystyle=x\cdot(yh_{2}\ldots h_{n}+h_{1}yh_{3}\ldots h_{n}+\ldots+h_{1}\ldots h_{n-1}y)

\displaystyle y\cdot\frac{\partial g}{\partial y}

\displaystyle=y\cdot(xh_{2}\ldots h_{n}+h_{1}xh_{3}\ldots h_{n}+\ldots+h_{1}\ldots h_{n-1}x)

which clearly satisfies
x\cdot\frac{\partial g}{\partial x}-y\cdot\frac{\partial g}{\partial y}=0.

∎

Lemma 3.10
.

For any scalars
\upalpha_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\upalpha_{2}\ldots\mathchar 59\relax\upalpha_{n}\mathchar 59\relax\upbeta_{2}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax\upbeta_{m}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
set
f_{1}\coloneqq ab-xy+\upalpha_{1}
and
f_{2}=1-a(ab-\upalpha_{2})\ldots(ab-\upalpha_{n})+x(xy-\upbeta_{2})\ldots(xy-\upbeta_{m}).
Then
\mathbb{C}[a\mathchar 59\relax b\mathchar 59\relax x\mathchar 59\relax y]/(f_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}~f_{2})\neq 0.

Proof.

Set
\mathcal{A}=\mathbb{C}[a\mathchar 59\relax b\mathchar 59\relax x\mathchar 59\relax y]/(f_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}~f_{2})\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
then we claim that
\mathcal{A}/(a-1)\neq 0.
Indeed,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{A}}{(a-1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{C}[b\mathchar 59\relax x\mathchar 59\relax y]}{\left(\begin{array}[]{c}b=xy-\upalpha_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\\
1-(b-\upalpha_{2})\ldots(b-\upalpha_{n})+x(xy-\upbeta_{2})\ldots(xy-\upbeta_{m})\end{array}\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{C}[x\mathchar 59\relax y]}{(1-(xy-(\upalpha_{1}+\upalpha_{2}))\ldots(xy-(\upalpha_{1}+\upalpha_{n}))+x(xy-\upbeta_{2})\ldots(xy-\upbeta_{m}))}\mathchar 59\relax

which is nonzero since the highest degree term is either
x^{n-1}y^{n-1}
or
x^{m}y^{m-1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
and these can never cancel.
∎

The following is the main result of this subsection.

Corollary 3.11
.

\mathcal{M}_{\upvartheta_{0}}(\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{\upgamma}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}~\updelta)
is two-dimensional and smooth.

Proof.

By Proposition
3.8
,
\Rep(\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{\upgamma}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}~\updelta)~/\!\!\!\!/_{\upvartheta_{0}}\mathrm{GL}
is covered by the open sets
U^{1}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}\mathchar 59\relax U^{2}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}\mathchar 59\relax U^{3}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
so it suffices to prove that each of these is two-dimensional and smooth.

Case 1.

Note that by Proposition
3.8
,
\mathbb{C}[U^{1}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}]\cong\mathbb{C}[d_{2i}\mathchar 59\relax u_{2i}\mathchar 59\relax d_{3j}\mathchar 59\relax u_{3j}]/(f_{1}\mathchar 59\relax f_{2})
where

\displaystyle f_{1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq d_{2i}u_{2i}+\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{i-1}\upgamma_{2l}-d_{3j}u_{3j}-\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{j-1}\upgamma_{3l}-b\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

\displaystyle f_{2}

\displaystyle\coloneqq 1-f_{21}+f_{22}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

\displaystyle f_{21}

\displaystyle\coloneqq d_{2i}(d_{2i}u_{2i}-\upgamma_{2i})\ldots(d_{2i}u_{2i}-\displaystyle\sum_{l=i}^{p_{2}-1}\upgamma_{2l})\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

\displaystyle f_{22}

\displaystyle\coloneqq d_{3j}(d_{3j}u_{3j}-\upgamma_{3j})\ldots(d_{3j}u_{3j}-\displaystyle\sum_{l=j}^{p_{3}-1}\upgamma_{3l}).

Since
\mathbb{C}[d_{2i}\mathchar 59\relax u_{2i}\mathchar 59\relax d_{3j}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}u_{3j}]
is an affine domain, and
f_{1}
is not a unit, then from
[
E
, Corollary 13.11]
,
S\coloneqq\mathbb{C}[d_{2i}\mathchar 59\relax u_{2i}\mathchar 59\relax d_{3j}\mathchar 59\relax u_{3j}]/(f_{1})
is a
3
-dimensional domain. Now
f_{2}
is not a unit in
S
since if it were a unit in
S\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
then

0=S/(f_{2})\cong\mathbb{C}[d_{2i}\mathchar 59\relax u_{2i}\mathchar 59\relax d_{3j}\mathchar 59\relax u_{3j}]/(f_{1}\mathchar 59\relax f_{2})

which would contradict Lemma
3.10
. Thus since
S
is an affine domain, again
[
E
, Corollary 13.11]
asserts that

\text{dim}~\mathbb{C}[U^{1}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}]=\text{dim}~S-1=2.

Therefore,
\text{dim}~\mathcal{M}_{\upvartheta_{0}}(\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{\upgamma}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}~\updelta)=2.

Now set
K=(f_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}~f_{2})\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
and consider Jacobian matrix

\mathcal{J}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\partial f_{1}}{\partial u_{2i}}&&\frac{\partial f_{1}}{\partial d_{2i}}&&\frac{\partial f_{1}}{\partial u_{3j}}&&\frac{\partial f_{1}}{\partial d_{3j}}\\
&&&&&&\\
\frac{\partial f_{2}}{\partial u_{2i}}&&\frac{\partial f_{2}}{\partial d_{2i}}&&\frac{\partial f_{2}}{\partial u_{3j}}&&\frac{\partial f_{2}}{\partial d_{3j}}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}d_{2i}&&u_{2i}&&-d_{3j}&&-u_{3j}\\
&&&&&&\\
\frac{\partial f_{2}}{\partial u_{2i}}&&\frac{\partial f_{2}}{\partial d_{2i}}&&\frac{\partial f_{2}}{\partial u_{3j}}&&\frac{\partial f_{2}}{\partial d_{3j}}\end{pmatrix}.

Write
\mathrm{J}
for the ideal generated by the
2\times 2
minors of
\mathcal{J}
, together with
K.
Then

\displaystyle f_{21}

\displaystyle=d_{2i}\cdot\frac{\partial f_{21}}{\partial d_{2i}}-u_{2i}\cdot\frac{\partial f_{21}}{\partial u_{2i}}

(by Lemma
3.9
)

\displaystyle=d_{2i}\cdot\frac{\partial f_{2}}{\partial d_{2i}}-u_{2i}\cdot\frac{\partial f_{2}}{\partial u_{2i}}

(by inspection)

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial f_{1}}{\partial u_{2i}}\cdot\frac{\partial f_{2}}{\partial d_{2i}}-\frac{\partial f_{1}}{\partial d_{2i}}\cdot\frac{\partial f_{2}}{\partial u_{2i}}\mathchar 59\relax

and thus
f_{21}\in\mathrm{J}.

With a similar argument as above, using the last
2\times 2
minor

\frac{\partial f_{1}}{\partial u_{3j}}\cdot\frac{\partial f_{2}}{\partial d_{3j}}-\frac{\partial f_{1}}{\partial d_{3j}}\cdot\frac{\partial f_{2}}{\partial u_{3j}}=\left(-d_{3j}\right)\cdot\frac{\partial f_{22}}{\partial d_{3j}}-\left(-u_{3j}\right)\cdot\frac{\partial f_{22}}{\partial u_{3j}}\mathchar 59\relax

together with Lemma
3.9
,

f_{22}=d_{3j}\cdot\frac{\partial f_{22}}{\partial d_{3j}}-u_{3j}\cdot\frac{\partial f_{22}}{\partial u_{3j}}\in\mathrm{J}.

Thus, since
f_{2}=1-f_{21}+f_{22}
is in
\mathrm{J}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
it follows that
1\in\mathrm{J}.
As is standard, see e.g
[
GP
, Algorithm 5.7.6]
, this implies that
\mathcal{M}_{\upvartheta_{0}}(\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{\upgamma}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}~\updelta)
is smooth.

Case 2.
The proof of
\mathbb{C}[U^{2}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}]
and
\mathbb{C}[U^{3}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}]
are similar to that of
\mathbb{C}[U^{1}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}]
in above.
∎

4.
Simultaneous Resolution

This section considers the invariant representation variety associated to the quiver of the reconstruction algebra in §
4.2
, and finds its generators in terms of cycles, the deformed reconstruction algebra introduced in §
3.3
is used to achieve simultaneous resolution.

4.1.
Representation Variety

Below, we say that arrows
p_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\ldots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}p_{n}
are
composable
if
h(p_{i})=t(p_{i+1})~\text{for all}~i=1\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax n-1.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
Q
be the quiver in
(
2.B
)
. With notation as in §
3.1
,
\mathcal{\mathcal{R}}^{G}
is generated by
p+I
where
p
is a cycle in
Q
.

Proof.

Choose a monomial
p=p_{1}\ldots p_{n}\in\mathcal{R}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
where
{p_{i}}
’s are arrows. We claim that

\upmu\cdot(p+I)=p~+I~\text{for all}~\upmu\Leftrightarrow p
is a cycle. First observe that
\upmu\cdot(p+I)=\upmu\cdot p+I=(\upmu_{t(p_{1})}\ldots\upmu_{t(p_{n})})^{-1}p(\upmu_{h(p_{1})}\ldots\upmu_{h(p_{n})})+I
.

(
\Leftarrow
) If
p
is a cycle, in particular it is composable. Thus for all
\upmu\in G\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

\displaystyle\upmu\cdot(p+I)

\displaystyle=\upmu\cdot p+I

\displaystyle=\upmu_{t(p_{1})}^{-1}p_{1}\upmu_{h(p_{1})}\upmu_{t(p_{2})}^{-1}p_{2}\upmu_{h(p_{2})}\ldots\upmu_{t(p_{n})}^{-1}p_{n}\upmu_{h(p_{n})}+I

\displaystyle=\upmu_{t(p_{1})}^{-1}\upmu_{h(p_{n})}p_{1}p_{2}\ldots p_{n}+I

\displaystyle=\upmu_{t(p_{1})}^{-1}\upmu_{h(p_{n})}p+I

\displaystyle=p+I.

(since
t(p_{1})=h(p_{n})
)

Hence
p+I\in\mathcal{R}^{G}
.

(
\Rightarrow
)
Suppose that
p+I\in\mathcal{R}^{G}
such that
\upmu\cdot(p+I)=\upmu\cdot p+I=p~+I
for all
\upmu
. Then
\upmu_{h(p_{1})}
must cancel some
\upmu_{t(p_{i})}^{-1}
for some
i
, so
h(p_{1})=t(p_{i}).
Now consider
\upmu_{h(p_{i})}.
It must cancel
\upmu_{t(p_{j})}^{-1}
for some
j
, so
h(p_{i})=t(p_{j}).

Continuing like this, we can assume
p=p_{1}p_{i}p_{j}\ldots p_{m}
where
p_{1}p_{i}p_{j}\ldots p_{m}
is composable.
But then
\upmu\cdot(p+I)=\upmu\cdot p+I=\upmu_{t(p_{1})}^{-1}\cdot p\cdot\upmu_{h(p_{m})}+I
and so since
\upmu\cdot(p+I)=p+I
,
t(p_{1})=h(p_{m})\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
and
p
is a cycle.
∎

4.2.
Reconstruction Algebras

By Lemma
4.1
,
\mathcal{R}^{G}
is generated by cycles. This subsection finds a finite generating set, by considering

\mathcal{S}_{1}\coloneqq\{2\textnormal{-cycles}\}\bigcup\left\{\begin{array}[]{cccc}D_{1}U_{1}\mathchar 59\relax&D_{1}U_{2}\mathchar 59\relax&D_{1}U_{3}\\
D_{2}U_{1}\mathchar 59\relax&D_{2}U_{2}\mathchar 59\relax&D_{2}U_{3}\\
D_{3}U_{1}\mathchar 59\relax&D_{3}U_{2}\mathchar 59\relax&D_{3}U_{3}\end{array}\right\}.

Proposition 4.2
.

\mathcal{R}^{G}
is generated as a
\mathbb{C}
–algebra by the set
\mathcal{S}_{1}.

Proof.

For any vertex
v
, consider a non trivial cycle
p
, then it must leave the vertex. According to the quiver, there are two options:

Case 1.
The path
p
starts with a downward arrow (
p=d_{ij}p^{\prime})
from vertex
v
along the
i^{th}
arm. Since
p
is a cycle,
p^{\prime}\colon h(d_{ij})\rightarrow v.
If
p^{\prime}
continues downwards, at some stage
p^{\prime}
stops travelling downwards, and we can write
p=d_{ij}d_{ij+1}\ldots d_{ik}p^{\prime}

for some
p^{\prime}\colon a\rightarrow v.
If
a\neq b\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
where
b
is the bottom vertex, then
p^{\prime}
starts upwards, so

p=d_{ij}\ldots\underbrace{(d_{ik}u_{ik})}_{z}p^{\prime\prime}\sim z\text{(cycles of length smaller than p)}

thus by induction,
p\in\left<\mathcal{S}_{1}\right>
. Hence we can assume
a=b\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
and
p^{\prime}
must travel up one of the arms. According to the quiver
Q
in §
2
, there are
3
options.

(a)

p^{\prime}
travels up the
i^{th}
arm. Again
p=d_{ij}\ldots(d_{ip_{i}}u_{ip_{i}})p^{\prime\prime}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
by induction we are done.

(b)

p^{\prime}
travels up one of the other
2
arms. If it doubles back on itself before reaching the top (
0^{th}
vertex), we are done by induction since
\mathcal{S}_{1}
contains all
2
-cycles. Hence we can assume
p^{\prime}
reaches the top, so
p=d_{ij}\ldots d_{ip_{i}}U_{l}p^{\prime\prime}
for some
l\neq i.
Repeating, either
p^{\prime\prime}
travels down the
i^{th}
arm without doubling back, in which case
p\sim D_{i}U_{l}p^{\prime\prime\prime}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
or
p^{\prime\prime}
travels down the
k^{th}
arm
(k\neq i)
without doubling back in which case
p\sim(d_{ij}\ldots d_{ip_{i}})(U_{l}D_{k})p^{\prime\prime}.
In either case, by induction we are done.

Case 2.

The path
p
starts with an upward arrow. This is very similar to Case 1,
after interchanging the downward and the upward arrows.
∎

Now, set

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{2}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{2\textnormal{-cycles}\}\bigcup\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccccc}&D_{1}U_{2}\mathchar 59\relax&D_{1}U_{3}\\
D_{2}U_{1}\mathchar 59\relax&&D_{2}U_{3}\\
\end{array}\right\}\mathchar 59\relax

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{3}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{2\textnormal{-cycles}\}\bigcup\,\,\{D_{1}U_{2}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}~D_{2}U_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}~D_{2}U_{3}\}.

Lemma 4.3
.

\left<\mathcal{S}_{3}\right>=\left<\mathcal{S}_{2}\right>=\left<\mathcal{S}_{1}\right>.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{S}_{3}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{2}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
it suffices to prove that
\left<\mathcal{S}_{1}\right>\subseteq\left<\mathcal{S}_{2}\right>\subseteq\left<\mathcal{S}_{3}\right>.

For
\left<\mathcal{S}_{1}\right>\subseteq\left<\mathcal{S}_{2}\right>\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
multiplying the relation
D_{3}=D_{2}-D_{1}
with
U_{1}\mathchar 59\relax U_{2}\mathchar 59\relax U_{3}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
shows that all elements in
\mathcal{S}_{1}
can be generated by the elements in
\mathcal{S}_{2}.

For
\left<\mathcal{S}_{2}\right>\subseteq\left<\mathcal{S}_{3}\right>\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
again
D_{3}U_{i}=D_{2}U_{i}-D_{1}U_{i}
for
i=1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}~2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}~3
and so
D_{1}U_{i}=D_{2}U_{i}-D_{3}U_{i}
where
D_{i}U_{i}
is a product of
2
-cycles. Therefore,
D_{3}U_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}~D_{3}U_{2}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}~D_{1}U_{3}
can be expressed in terms of elements of
\mathcal{S}_{3}.

∎

4.3.
Simultaneous Resolution

Recall from
(
3.A
) that
\mathcal{\mathcal{R}}=\mathbb{C}[\Rep(\mathbb{C}Q\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\updelta)]/I\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
and
G=\mathrm{GL}
acts on
\mathcal{\mathcal{R}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
so we can form
\mathcal{\mathcal{R}}^{G}.
By §
4.2
,
\mathcal{\mathcal{R}}^{G}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathsf{w}_{1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq D_{1}U_{2}+I

\displaystyle\mathsf{w}_{2}

\displaystyle\coloneqq~~D_{2}U_{1}+I

\displaystyle\mathsf{w}_{3}

\displaystyle\coloneqq-D_{2}U_{3}+I

\displaystyle\mathsf{v}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}

\displaystyle\coloneqq~~d_{ij}u_{ij}+I.

for
i=1\mathchar 59\relax 2\mathchar 59\relax 3~\text{and}~1<j\leq p_{i}.

Write
(\upbeta_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\upbeta_{2}\mathchar 59\relax\upbeta_{3}\mathchar 59\relax\upalpha_{1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\ldots\mathchar 59\relax\upalpha_{1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}p_{1}}\mathchar 59\relax\upalpha_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\ldots\mathchar 59\relax\upalpha_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}p_{2}}\mathchar 59\relax\upalpha_{3\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\ldots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

\upalpha_{3\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}p_{3}})
for the point in Spec
\mathcal{\mathcal{R}}^{G}
corresponding to the maximal ideal
(\mathsf{w}_{1}-\upbeta_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\mathsf{w}_{2}-\upbeta_{2}\mathchar 59\relax\mathsf{w}_{3}-\upbeta_{3}\mathchar 59\relax\mathsf{v}_{1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}-\upalpha_{1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\ldots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\mathsf{v}_{3\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}p_{3}}-\upalpha_{3\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}p_{3}}).
Let
Q
be the quiver of the reconstruction algebra, and consider the map

\uppi\colon\mathcal{\mathcal{R}}^{G}=\Rep(\mathbb{C}Q/I\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}~\updelta)~/\!\!\!\!/~\mathrm{GL}\rightarrow\Delta\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

defined by taking

(\upbeta_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\upbeta_{2}\mathchar 59\relax\upbeta_{3}\mathchar 59\relax\upalpha_{1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\ldots\mathchar 59\relax\upalpha_{1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}p_{1}}\mathchar 59\relax\upalpha_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\ldots\mathchar 59\relax\upalpha_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}p_{2}}\mathchar 59\relax\upalpha_{3\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\ldots\mathchar 59\relax\upalpha_{3\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}p_{3}})
((\upalpha_{1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}i}-\upalpha_{1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}i+1})^{p_{1}-1}_{i=1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}(\upalpha_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}i}-\upalpha_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}i+1})^{p_{2}-1}_{i=1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}(\upalpha_{3\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}i}-\upalpha_{3\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}i+1})^{p_{3}-1}_{i=1}\mathchar 59\relax\upalpha_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}-\upalpha_{1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}\mathchar 59\relax\upalpha_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}-\upalpha_{3\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}\mathchar 59\relax\upalpha_{1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}p_{1}}-\upalpha_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}p_{2}}\mathchar 59\relax\upalpha_{3\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}p_{3}}-\upalpha_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}p_{2}}).

Remark 4.4
.

The fibre above a point
\boldsymbol{\upgamma}\in\Delta
is precisely
\Rep(\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{\upgamma}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}~\updelta)/\!\!/\mathrm{GL}.
Indeed, the fibre above
\boldsymbol{\upgamma}\in\Delta
is the zero locus of

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}(1)&~\mathsf{v}_{1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}i}-\mathsf{v}_{1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}i+1}&=&\upgamma_{1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}i}&~\text{for all}~1\leq i\leq p_{1}-1\\
(2)&~\mathsf{v}_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}i}-\mathsf{v}_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}i+1}&=&\upgamma_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}i}&~\text{for all}~1\leq i\leq p_{2}-1\\
(3)&~\mathsf{v}_{3\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}i}-\mathsf{v}_{3\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}i+1}&=&\upgamma_{3\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}i}&~\text{for all}~1\leq i\leq p_{3}-1\\
(a)&~\mathsf{v}_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}-\mathsf{v}_{1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}&=&a&\\
(b)&~\mathsf{v}_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}-\mathsf{v}_{3\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}&=&b&\\
(c)&~\mathsf{v}_{1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}p_{1}}-\mathsf{v}_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}p_{2}}&=&A&\\
(d)&~\mathsf{v}_{3\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}p_{3}}-\mathsf{v}_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}p_{2}}&=&B&\end{array}

By Definition
3.4
, this equals
\Rep(\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{\upgamma}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}~\updelta)/\!\!/\mathrm{GL}.
In particular, the fibre above the origin is
\Rep(\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{0}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}~\updelta)/\!\!/\mathrm{GL}.
Since
\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{0}}
is the (undeformed) reconstruction algebra, this is known to be the determinantal singularity corresponding to (
2.A
).

The following is the main result of this paper.

Theorem 4.5
.

The diagram

\Rep(\mathbb{C}Q/I\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}~\updelta)~/\!\!\!\!/_{\upvartheta_{0}}\mathrm{GL}
\Rep(\mathbb{C}Q/I\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}~\updelta)~/\!\!\!\!/~\mathrm{GL}
\Delta
\upphi
\uppi

is a simultaneous resolution of singularities in the sense that the morphism
\upphi
is smooth, and
\uppi
is flat.

Proof.

Write
\upphi
for the composition

Y=\Rep(\mathbb{C}Q/I\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}~\updelta)~/\!\!\!\!/_{\upvartheta_{0}}\mathrm{GL}\rightarrow\Rep(\mathbb{C}Q/I\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}~\updelta)~/\!\!\!\!/~\mathrm{GL}\rightarrow\Delta.

We first claim that
\upphi
is flat. Since
(1)~\Delta
is regular,
(2)~Y
is regular (so Cohen-Macaulay) by
3.3
,
(3)~\mathbb{C}
is algebraically closed so
\upphi
takes closed points of
Y
to closed points of
\Delta\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
and
(4)
for every closed point
\boldsymbol{\upgamma}\in\Delta\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
for the same reason as in Remark
4.4
the fibre
\upphi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\upgamma})
is
\Rep(\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{\upgamma}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}~\updelta)~/\!\!\!\!/_{\upvartheta_{0}}\mathrm{GL}
which is always two-dimensional by Corollary
3.11
, it follows from
[
M2
, Corollary to 23.1]
that
\upphi
is flat.

Now as in
[
L
, 3.35]
to show that
\upphi
is smooth, we just require smoothness (equivalently regularity, as we are working over
\mathbb{C}
) at closed points of fibres above closed points
\boldsymbol{\upgamma}\in\Delta.
But as above
\upphi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\upgamma})
is
\Rep(\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{\upgamma}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}~\updelta)~/\!\!\!\!/_{\upvartheta_{0}}\mathrm{GL}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
which is regular at all closed points by Corollary
3.11
. Thus
\upphi
is a smooth morphism, as required.

Finally, the above can be adapted to show that
\uppi
is flat. We have that
\uppi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\upgamma})=\Rep(\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{\upgamma}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}~\updelta)/\!\!/\mathrm{GL}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
which is always two-dimensional as a consequence of the resolution of its singularities computed in Corollary
3.11
. Thus we can still appeal to
[
M2
, Corollary to 23.1]
.
∎

5.
The Representation Variety in Determinantal Form

This section gives a conjectural explicit presentation of the high-dimensional invariant representation variety
\mathcal{\mathcal{R}}^{G}=\Rep(\mathbb{C}Q/I\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}~\updelta)/\!\!/\mathrm{GL}
in §
3
. Although not strictly needed for simultaneous resolution in §
4
, it does link to other work in the literature.

5.1.
Determinantal form

Consider a
2\times n
matrix

X=\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}&&a_{2}&&\ldots&&a_{n}\\
b_{1}&&b_{2}&&\ldots&&b_{n}\\
\end{pmatrix}

Following Stevens
[
S
, §12]
, consider the
2\times 2

minors
of this
2\times n

matrix
, which for all,
i<j
are defined to be

a_{i}\cdot b_{j}-b_{i}\cdot a_{j}.

Write
\mathrm{Det}(X)
for the set of all
2\times 2

minors
of
X
.

Consider the natural homomorphism

\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{w}\mathchar 59\relax\mathsf{v}]\xrightarrow{\varphi}\mathcal{R}^{G}=\Rep(\mathbb{C}Q/I\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}~\updelta)/\!\!/\mathrm{GL}\mathchar 59\relax

defined by taking

\displaystyle\mathsf{w}_{1}

\displaystyle\mapsto D_{1}U_{2}+I

\displaystyle\mathsf{w}_{2}

\displaystyle\mapsto~~D_{2}U_{1}+I

\displaystyle\mathsf{w}_{3}

\displaystyle\mapsto-D_{2}U_{3}+I

\displaystyle\mathsf{v}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}

\displaystyle\mapsto~~d_{ij}u_{ij}+I.

for
i=1\mathchar 59\relax 2\mathchar 59\relax 3~\text{and}~1<j\leq p_{i}.

Proposition 5.1
.

The homomorphism
\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{w}\mathchar 59\relax\mathsf{v}]\xrightarrow{\varphi}\mathcal{R}^{G}
is surjective, and all the
2\times 2
minors of the matrix

\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}{\mathsf{w}}_{2}&{\mathsf{w}}_{3}&{v_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}\ldots v_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}{p_{2}}}}\\
{v_{1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}\ldots v_{1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}{p_{1}}}}&{\mathsf{w}}_{3}+{v_{3\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}\ldots v_{3\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}{p_{3}}}}&{\mathsf{w}}_{1}\end{array}\right)

(5.A)

are all sent to zero.

Proof.

Surjectivity follows from Lemma
4.3
. The
2\times 2
minor of the outer columns gives

\varphi(\mathsf{w}_{2}\mathsf{w}_{1}-(\mathsf{v}_{1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}\ldots\mathsf{v}_{1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}{p_{1}}})(\mathsf{v}_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}\ldots\mathsf{v}_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}{p_{2}}}))=D_{2}U_{1}D_{1}U_{2}-D_{1}U_{1}D_{2}U_{2}=0

Using
D_{1}U_{i}=D_{2}U_{i}-D_{3}U_{i}
from the relation
x_{1}^{p_{1}}=x_{2}^{p_{2}}-x_{3}^{p_{3}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
we further have

\displaystyle\varphi(\mathsf{w}_{1}\mathsf{w}_{3})

\displaystyle=(D_{1}U_{2})(-D_{2}U_{3})

\displaystyle=-(D_{1}U_{3})(D_{2}U_{2})

\displaystyle=(-D_{2}U_{3}+D_{3}U_{3})D_{2}U_{2}

\displaystyle=\varphi((\mathsf{w}_{3}+\mathsf{v}_{3\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}\ldots\mathsf{v}_{3\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}p_{3}})(\mathsf{v}_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}\ldots\mathsf{v}_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}p_{2}})).

Thus the
2\times 2
minor consisting of the
i^{th}
column and the last one is sent to zero.

Finally,

\displaystyle\varphi(\mathsf{w}_{2}(\mathsf{w}_{3}+\mathsf{v}_{3\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}\ldots\mathsf{v}_{3\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}p_{3}}))

\displaystyle=(D_{2}U_{1})(\mathsf{w}_{3}+\mathsf{v}_{3\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}\ldots\mathsf{v}_{3\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}p_{3}})

\displaystyle=(D_{2}U_{1})(-D_{2}U_{3}+D_{3}U_{3})

\displaystyle=(D_{2}U_{1})\cdot-(D_{1}U_{3})

\displaystyle=(D_{1}U_{1})(-D_{2}U_{3})

\displaystyle=\varphi((\mathsf{v}_{1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}\ldots\mathsf{v}_{1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}p_{1}})\mathsf{w}_{3}).

This shows that the
2\times 2
minors of the matrix belong to the kernel
\varphi\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
as required.
∎

Conjecture 5.2
.

The ring homomorphism
\varphi
is an isomorphism.

Calculations in magma
[
BCP
]
confirm this in small cases, but the Gröbner approach seems hard in general.
